27: +Management is transparent: the IPv4 and IPv6 addresses and gateways containerlab gives the node's `eth0` are configured on RouterOS `ether1`, and the container's `eth0` is bridged to the VM's `ether1`. The router is the node's management address: SSH, the REST API, Winbox, SNMP and every other service answer on it, and the address in the topology table is the one in the RouterOS configuration.
28:  
29:  The image ships busybox only (no `tc`), so where vrnetlab's [transparent management](../vrnetlab.md#management-interface) redirects with tc-mirred, this kind uses a Linux bridge. The container itself has no address on the management network; it reaches RouterOS for bootstrap over IPv6 link-local on that bridge.
30:  
31:  The management return path lives in its own routing table, `mgmt`, selected by routing rules for the management addresses, and CHR's `ether1` DHCP client is disabled. A default route in the lab configuration, a PPPoE client's default route or a dual-WAN setup in `main` therefore does not cut SSH or REST access to the node. RouterOS's router advertisements on `ether1` carry a zero router lifetime, so no host on the management network takes the router as its IPv6 default gateway; the container joins the management network only once this is in place. The objects carry the comment `clab-mgmt` and are re-applied at boot when the container's addressing changed.
32:  
33:  /// warning
34:  Do not remove or change the `clab-mgmt` objects or the `mgmt` routing table: management of the node depends on them.
35:  ///
36:  
37: +### Neighbor discovery
38: +
39: +CHR routers sharing the management bridge can appear in each other's neighbor tables. LLDP, CDP and MNDP are disabled on management `ether1` by default; discovery stays enabled on the other static interfaces.
40: +
41: +On first boot, before joining the management network, the launcher creates:
42: +
43: +```routeros
44: +/interface list
45: +add name=clab-management comment=clab-mgmt
46: +add name=clab-discovery include=static exclude=clab-management comment=clab-mgmt
47: +/interface list member
48: +add list=clab-management interface=ether1 comment=clab-mgmt
49: +/ip neighbor discovery-settings
50: +set discover-interface-list=clab-discovery
51: +```
52: +
53: +These reserved lists belong to the launcher. To change discovery, select another list in your startup configuration or on the running router. For example, to enable discovery on all static interfaces, including management:
54: +
55: +```routeros
56: +/ip neighbor discovery-settings set discover-interface-list=static
57: +```
58: +
59: +Use `discover-interface-list=none` to disable discovery everywhere, or select your own interface list. Startup configuration is imported after this default is applied. Restarts preserve the selected policy, including on nodes created before this default was introduced. SSH, REST and other management services remain available. This setting does not control IPv6 address resolution or router advertisements under `/ipv6 nd`.
60: +
61: +As with RouterOS's normal discovery rules, including a bridge or bonding master enables discovery on its slave ports. If your configuration puts `ether1` into a bridge or bond, select a discovery list that excludes the master as well.
62: +
63:  ## Restarts
64:  
65:  [`containerlab restart`](../../cmd/restart.md) and a `/system reboot` inside RouterOS keep the interface names, MAC addresses, configuration and links: the launcher persists the NIC identity in the container on first boot and does not re-run the bootstrap. A plain `docker restart` keeps the router and its management, but Docker deletes the node's veth links, which stay down until the node is restarted with containerlab or the lab is redeployed.
66:

This setting does not control IPv6 address resolution or router advertisements under `/ipv6 nd`.\n60: +\n61: +As with RouterOS's normal discovery rules, including a bridge or bonding master enables discovery on its slave ports. The router is the node's management address: SSH, the REST API, Winbox, SNMP and every other service answer on it, and the address in the topology table is the one in the RouterOS configuration.\n \n The image ships busybox only (no `tc`), so where vrnetlab's [transparent management](../vrnetlab.md#management-interface) redirects with tc-mirred, this kind uses a Linux bridge. The container itself has no address on the management network; it reaches RouterOS for bootstrap over IPv6 link-local on that bridge.\n \n The management return path lives in its own routing table, `mgmt`, selected by routing rules for the management addresses, and CHR's `ether1` DHCP client is disabled. A default route in the lab configuration, a PPPoE client's default route or a dual-WAN setup in `main` therefore does not cut SSH or REST access to the node. RouterOS's router advertisements on `ether1` carry a zero router lifetime, so no host on the management network takes the router as its IPv6 default gateway; the container joins the management network only once this is in place. The objects carry the comment `clab-mgmt` and are re-applied at boot when the container's addressing changed.\n \n /// warning\n Do not remove or change the `clab-mgmt` objects or the `mgmt` routing table: management of the node depends on them.\n ///\n \n+### Neighbor discovery\n+\n+CHR routers sharing the management bridge can appear in each other's neighbor tables. LLDP, CDP and MNDP are disabled on management `ether1` by default; discovery stays enabled on the other static interfaces.\n+\n+On first boot, before joining the management network, the launcher creates:\n+\n+```routeros\n+/interface list\n+add name=clab-management comment=clab-mgmt\n+add name=clab-discovery include=static exclude=clab-management comment=clab-mgmt\n+/interface list member\n+add list=clab-management interface=ether1 comment=clab-mgmt\n+/ip neighbor discovery-settings\n+set discover-interface-list=clab-discovery\n+```\n+\n+These reserved lists belong to the launcher. To change discovery, select another list in your startup configuration or on the running router. For example, to enable discovery on all static interfaces, including management:\n+\n+```routeros\n+/ip neighbor discovery-settings set discover-interface-list=static\n+```\n+\n+Use `discover-interface-list=none` to disable discovery everywhere, or select your own interface list. Startup configuration is imported after this default is applied. Restarts preserve the selected policy, including on nodes created before this default was introduced. SSH, REST and other management services remain available. This setting does not control IPv6 address resolution or router advertisements under `/ipv6 nd`.\n+\n+As with RouterOS's normal discovery rules, including a bridge or bonding master enables discovery on its slave ports. <path>/source/standards/karpathy-guidelines.mdc</path>
<type>file</type>
<content>
1: ---
2: description: Behavioral guidelines to reduce common LLM coding mistakes. Use when writing, reviewing, or refactoring code to avoid overcomplication, make surgical changes, surface assumptions, and define verifiable success criteria.
3: alwaysApply: true
4: ---
5: 
6: # Karpathy behavioral guidelines
7: 
8: Behavioral guidelines to reduce common LLM coding mistakes. Merge with project-specific instructions as needed.
9: 
10: **Tradeoff:** These guidelines bias toward caution over speed. For trivial tasks, use judgment.
11: 
12: ## 1. Think Before Coding
13: 
14: **Don't assume. Don't hide confusion. Surface tradeoffs.**
15: 
16: Before implementing:
17: 
18: - State your assumptions explicitly. If uncertain, ask.
19: - If multiple interpretations exist, present them - don't pick silently.
20: - If a simpler approach exists, say so. Push back when warranted.
21: - If something is unclear, stop. Name what's confusing. Ask.
22: 
23: ## 2. Simplicity First
24: 
25: **Minimum code that solves the problem. Nothing speculative.**
26: 
27: - No features beyond what was asked.
28: - No abstractions for single-use code.
29: - No "flexibility" or "configurability" that wasn't requested.
30: - No error handling for impossible scenarios.
31: - If you write 200 lines and it could be 50, rewrite it.
32: 
33: Ask yourself: "Would a senior engineer say this is overcomplicated?" If yes, simplify.
34: 
35: ## 3. Surgical Changes
36: 
37: **Touch only what you must. Clean up only your own mess.**
38: 
39: When editing existing code:
40: 
41: - Don't "improve" adjacent code, comments, or formatting.
42: - Don't refactor things that aren't broken.
43: - Match existing style, even if you'd do it differently.
44: - If you notice unrelated dead code, mention it - don't delete it.
45: 
46: When your changes create orphans:
47: 
48: - Remove imports/variables/functions that YOUR changes made unused.
49: - Don't remove pre-existing dead code unless asked.
50: 
51: The test: Every changed line should trace directly to the user's request.
52: 
53: ## 4. Goal-Driven Execution
54: 
55: **Define success criteria. Loop until verified.**
56: 
57: Transform tasks into verifiable goals:
58: 
59: - "Add validation" → "Write tests for invalid inputs, then make them pass"
60: - "Fix the bug" → "Write a test that reproduces it, then make it pass"
61: - "Refactor X" → "Ensure tests pass before and after"
62: 
63: For multi-step tasks, state a brief plan:
64: 
65: ```
66: 1. [Step] → verify: [check]
67: 2. [Step] → verify: [check]
68: 3. [Step] → verify: [check]
69: ```
70: 
71: Strong success criteria let you loop independently. Weak criteria ("make it work") require constant clarification.
72: 
73: ---
74: 
75: **These guidelines are working if:** fewer unnecessary changes in diffs, fewer rewrites due to overcomplication, and clarifying questions come before implementation rather than after mistakes.

(End of file - total 75 lines)
</content>

<path>/source/standards/ponytail.mdc</path>
<type>file</type>
<content>
1: ---
2: description: Ponytail, lazy senior dev mode. Always pick the simplest solution that works.
3: globs:
4: alwaysApply: true
5: ---
6: 
7: # Ponytail, lazy senior dev mode
8: 
9: You are a lazy senior developer. Lazy means efficient, not careless. The best code is the code never written.
10: 
11: Before writing any code, stop at the first rung that holds:
12: 
13: 1. Does this need to be built at all? (YAGNI)
14: 2. Does it already exist in this codebase? Reuse the helper, util, or pattern that's already here, don't re-write it.
15: 3. Does the standard library already do this? Use it.
16: 4. Does a native platform feature cover it? Use it.
17: 5. Does an already-installed dependency solve it? Use it.
18: 6. Can this be one line? Make it one line.
19: 7. Only then: write the minimum code that works.
20: 
21: The ladder runs after you understand the problem, not instead of it: read the task and the code it touches, trace the real flow end to end, then climb.
22: 
23: Bug fix = root cause, not symptom: a report names a symptom. Grep every caller of the function you touch and fix the shared function once — one guard there is a smaller diff than one per caller, and patching only the path the ticket names leaves a sibling caller still broken.
24: 
25: Rules:
26: 
27: - No abstractions that weren't explicitly requested.
28: - No new dependency if it can be avoided.
29: - No boilerplate nobody asked for.
30: - Deletion over addition. Boring over clever. Fewest files possible.
31: - Shortest working diff wins, but only once you understand the problem. The smallest change in the wrong place isn't lazy, it's a second bug.
32: - Question complex requests: "Do you actually need X, or does Y cover it?"
33: - Pick the edge-case-correct option when two stdlib approaches are the same size, lazy means less code, not the flimsier algorithm.
34: - Mark deliberate simplifications that cut a real corner with a known ceiling (global lock, O(n²) scan, naive heuristic) with a `ponytail:` comment naming the ceiling and upgrade path.
35: 
36: Not lazy about: understanding the problem (read it fully and trace the real flow before picking a rung, a small diff you don't understand is just laziness dressed up as efficiency), input validation at trust boundaries, error handling that prevents data loss, security, accessibility, the calibration real hardware needs (the platform is never the spec ideal, a clock drifts, a sensor reads off), anything explicitly requested. Lazy code without its check is unfinished: non-trivial logic leaves ONE runnable check behind, the smallest thing that fails if the logic breaks (an assert-based demo/self-check or one small test file; no frameworks, no fixtures). Trivial one-liners need no test.

(End of file - total 36 lines)
</content>

The smallest change in the wrong place isn't lazy, it's a second bug.\n32: - Question complex requests: \"Do you actually need X, or does Y cover it?\"\n33: - Pick the edge-case-correct option when two stdlib approaches are the same size, lazy means less code, not the flimsier algorithm.\n34: - Mark deliberate simplifications that cut a real corner with a known ceiling (global lock, O(n²) scan, naive heuristic) with a `ponytail:` comment naming the ceiling and upgrade path.\n35: \n36: Not lazy about: understanding the problem (read it fully and trace the real flow before picking a rung, a small diff you don't understand is just laziness dressed up as efficiency), input validation at trust boundaries, error handling that prevents data loss, security, accessibility, the calibration real hardware needs (the platform is never the spec ideal, a clock drifts, a sensor reads off), anything explicitly requested. Lazy code without its check is unfinished: non-trivial logic leaves ONE runnable check behind, the smallest thing that fails if the logic breaks (an assert-based demo/self-check or one small test file; no frameworks, no fixtures). Trivial one-liners need no test.\n\n(End of file - total 36 lines)\n</content>",

The best code is the code never written.\n\nBefore writing any code, stop at the first rung that holds:\n\n1. Does this need to be built at all? (YAGNI)\n2. Does it already exist in this codebase? Reuse the helper, util, or pattern that's already here, don't re-write it.\n3. Does the standard library already do this? Use it.\n4. Does a native platform feature cover it? Use it.\n5. 1: // Copyright 2026 NetPilot Labs
2: // Licensed under the BSD 3-Clause License.
3: // SPDX-License-Identifier: BSD-3-Clause
4: 
5: // Package mikrotik_chr runs MikroTik RouterOS CHR from the official mikrotik/chr image.
6: //
7: // The image is MikroTik's own distribution (Alpine + qemu + the CHR disk) and is used
8: // unmodified: the kind writes chr-launch.sh into the node's lab directory, bind-mounts it
9: // read-only and sets it as the entrypoint in place of the image's /start.sh, which is not
10: // orchestration-ready (containerlab#3116, #3464).
11: package mikrotik_chr
12: 
13: import (
14: 	"context"
15: 	_ "embed"
16: 	"fmt"
17: 	"net"
18: 	"os"
19: 	"path"

21: 	"regexp"
22: 	"slices"
23: 	"strconv"

26: 	clabconstants "github.com/srl-labs/containerlab/constants"
27: 	clabnodes "github.com/srl-labs/containerlab/nodes"
28: 	clabtypes "github.com/srl-labs/containerlab/types"
29: 	clabutils "github.com/srl-labs/containerlab/utils"
30: )
31: 
32: var (
33: 	kindNames          = []string{"mikrotik_chr"}
34: 	defaultCredentials = clabnodes.NewCredentials("admin", "admin")
35: 
36: 	InterfaceRegexp = regexp.MustCompile(`ether(?P<port>\d+)`)
37: 	InterfaceOffset = 2
38: 	InterfaceHelp   = "etherX (where X >= 2) or ethX (where X >= 1)"
39: 
40: 	//go:embed chr-launch.sh
41: 	launcherScript []byte
42: )
43: 
44: const (
45: 	configDirName   = "ftpboot"
46: 	startupCfgFName = "config.auto.rsc"
47: 
48: 	launcherFName = "chr-launch.sh"
49: 	launcherPath  = "/chr-launch.sh"
50: 	// readyFile is written by the launcher once RouterOS accepted the credentials and the
51: 	// startup config, and removed at every container start.
52: 	readyFile = "/.chr-launch-ready"
53: 
54: 	// mgmtComment tags every management object the launcher creates in RouterOS.
55: 	mgmtComment = "clab-mgmt"
56: 
57: 	// the launcher's CHR_INTF_WAIT and CHR_BOOT_WAIT defaults, and the time its config
58: 	// fetch and gateway check add, in seconds.
59: 	defaultIntfWait  = 60
60: 	defaultBootWait  = 300
61: 	startPeriodSlack = 90
62: 
63: 	scrapliPlatformName = "mikrotik_routeros" //nolint: misspell
64: )
65: 
66: // Register registers the node in the NodeRegistry.
67: func Register(r *clabnodes.NodeRegistry) {
68: 	platformAttrs := &clabnodes.PlatformAttrs{
69: 		ScrapliPlatformName: scrapliPlatformName,
70: 	}
71: 
72: 	nrea := clabnodes.NewNodeRegistryEntryAttributes(defaultCredentials, nil, platformAttrs)
73: 
74: 	r.Register(kindNames, func() clabnodes.Node {
75: 		return new(mikrotikCHR)
76: 	}, nrea)
77: }
78: 
79: type mikrotikCHR struct {
80: 	clabnodes.VRNode
81: }
82: 
83: func (n *mikrotikCHR) Init(cfg *clabtypes.NodeConfig, opts ...clabnodes.NodeOption) error {
84: 	n.VRNode = *clabnodes.NewVRNode(n, defaultCredentials, scrapliPlatformName)
85: 	n.ConfigDirName = configDirName
86: 	n.StartupCfgFName = startupCfgFName
87: 	// The image's qemu falls back to TCG emulation without /dev/kvm: KVM makes the boot
88: 	// fast but is not required.
89: 	n.HostRequirements.VirtRequired = false
90: 
91: 	n.Cfg = cfg
92: 	for _, o := range opts {
93: 		o(n)
94: 	}
95: 
96: 	// Credentials travel as env, not on the command line, so a password needs no quoting.
97: 	defEnv := map[string]string{
98: 		"USERNAME": n.Cfg.Credentials.Username,
99: 		"PASSWORD": n.Cfg.Credentials.Password,
100: 	}
101: 	n.Cfg.Env = clabutils.MergeStringMaps(defEnv, n.Cfg.Env)
102: 
103: 	n.Cfg.Binds = append(n.Cfg.Binds,
104: 		fmt.Sprint(path.Join(n.Cfg.LabDir, configDirName), ":/ftpboot"),
105: 		fmt.Sprint(path.Join(n.Cfg.LabDir, launcherFName), ":", launcherPath, ":ro"),
106: 	)
107: 
108: 	if n.Cfg.Entrypoint == "" {
109: 		n.Cfg.Entrypoint = "/bin/sh " + launcherPath
110: 	}
111: 	n.Cfg.Cmd = fmt.Sprintf("--hostname %s", n.Cfg.ShortName)
112: 
113: 	if n.Cfg.Healthcheck == nil {
114: 		n.Cfg.Healthcheck = &clabtypes.HealthcheckConfig{
115: 			Test:        []string{"CMD-SHELL", "test -f " + readyFile},
116: 			Interval:    5,
117: 			Timeout:     3,
118: 			Retries:     3,
119: 			StartPeriod: startPeriod(n.Cfg.Env),
120: 		}
121: 	}
122: 
123: 	n.InterfaceRegexp = InterfaceRegexp
124: 	n.InterfaceOffset = InterfaceOffset
125: 	n.InterfaceHelp = InterfaceHelp
126: 
127: 	return nil
128: }
129: 
130: // startPeriod is the healthcheck start period in seconds: the launcher's own worst case
131: // before the ready file, so a slow boot (TCG) never reports unhealthy. It waits up to
132: // CHR_INTF_WAIT for the links, then up to CHR_BOOT_WAIT each for REST, the startup-config
133: // import and the SSH banner, plus the config fetch and the gateway check.
134: func startPeriod(env map[string]string) int {
135: 	return envSeconds(env, "CHR_INTF_WAIT", defaultIntfWait) +
136: 		3*envSeconds(env, "CHR_BOOT_WAIT", defaultBootWait) + startPeriodSlack
137: }
138: 
139: // envSeconds reads a positive number of seconds from env, else returns def (the launcher
140: // falls back to its default the same way).
141: func envSeconds(env map[string]string, key string, def int) int {
142: 	if v, err := strconv.Atoi(strings.TrimSpace(env[key])); err == nil && v > 0 {
143: 		return v
144: 	}
145: 
146: 	return def
147: }
148: 
149: // PreDeploy writes the launcher next to the startup config on every deploy, so a
150: // containerlab upgrade ships its launcher to existing labs.
151: func (n *mikrotikCHR) PreDeploy(ctx context.Context, params *clabnodes.PreDeployParams) error {
152: 	if err := n.VRNode.PreDeploy(ctx, params); err != nil {
153: 		return err
154: 	}
155: 
156: 	dst := filepath.Join(n.Cfg.LabDir, launcherFName)
157: 	if err := os.WriteFile(dst, launcherScript, clabconstants.PermissionsFileDefault); err != nil {
158: 		return fmt.Errorf("node %q: failed to write %s: %w", n.Cfg.ShortName, dst, err)
159: 	}
160: 
161: 	return nil
162: }
163: 
164: // PreStop skips VRNode's vrnetlab qcow2 alias step, which does not apply to this image.
165: func (*mikrotikCHR) PreStop(_ context.Context) error {
166: 	return nil
167: }
168: 
169: // SaveConfig exports the RouterOS configuration over SSH (scrapligo has no RouterOS
170: // platform, see clabnodes.ExportConfigOverSSH) and writes it as the node's startup config,
171: // without the objects the launcher creates at every boot.
172: func (n *mikrotikCHR) SaveConfig(_ context.Context) (*clabnodes.SaveConfigResult, error) {
173: 	exported, err := clabnodes.ExportConfigOverSSH(
174: 		net.JoinHostPort(n.Cfg.LongName, "22"),
175: 		n.Cfg.Credentials.Username,
176: 		n.Cfg.Credentials.Password,
177: 		// A normal export omits RouterOS defaults, including an explicit opt-in
178: 		// to discovery on "static". Preserve that choice on the next deployment.
179: 		"/export; /ip neighbor discovery-settings export verbose",
180: 	)
181: 	if err != nil {
182: 		return nil, err
183: 	}
184: 
185: 	return clabnodes.WriteSavedConfig(n.Cfg, n.ConfigDirName, n.StartupCfgFName,
186: 		[]byte(filterLauncherConfig(exported)+"\n"))
187: }
188: 
189: // filterLauncherConfig drops what the launcher configures at every boot from an /export:
190: // every object tagged clab-mgmt (the ether1 management addresses, the mgmt routing table,
191: // its routes and rules, and the discovery lists), the ether1 ND entry and CHR's ether1
192: // DHCP client. Importing these lines as a startup config duplicates objects the launcher
193: // already created and
194: // RouterOS stops the import at the first one; the management addressing also belongs to
195: // the deployment, not the lab. Continuation lines (an unescaped trailing backslash) are joined
196: // before matching.
197: func filterLauncherConfig(config string) string {
198: 	var out []string
199: 
200: 	section := ""
201: 	pending := ""
202: 	for line := range strings.SplitSeq(strings.ReplaceAll(config, "\r\n", "\n"), "\n") {
203: 		if pending != "" {
204: 			line = pending + "\n" + line
205: 			pending = ""
206: 		}
207: 		if continues(line) {
208: 			pending = line
209: 			continue
210: 		}
211: 
212: 		trimmed := strings.TrimSpace(line)
213: 		if strings.HasPrefix(trimmed, "/") {
214: 			section = trimmed
215: 		}
216: 		if launcherOwned(section, trimmed) {
217: 			continue
218: 		}
219: 		out = append(out, line)
220: 	}
221: 	if pending != "" {
222: 		out = append(out, pending)
223: 	}
224: 
225: 	return strings.Join(out, "\n")
226: }
227: 
228: // continues reports whether an /export line continues on the next one: it ends in an
229: // unescaped backslash. RouterOS writes a literal backslash in a value as `\\`, so an even
230: // run of trailing backslashes ends the line.
231: func continues(line string) bool {
232: 	trailing := len(line) - len(strings.TrimRight(line, `\`))
233: 
234: 	return trailing%2 == 1
235: }
236: 
237: func launcherOwned(section, line string) bool {
238: 	if !strings.HasPrefix(line, "add ") {
239: 		return false
240: 	}
241: 
242: 	fields := strings.Fields(strings.ReplaceAll(line, `\`, " "))
243: 	has := func(kv string) bool { return slices.Contains(fields, kv) }
244: 
245: 	if has("comment=" + mgmtComment) {
246: 		return true
247: 	}
248: 
249: 	// /ipv6 nd keeps no comment: the launcher's ether1 entry is matched by interface.
250: 	switch section {
251: 	case "/ip address", "/ipv6 address", "/ip dhcp-client", "/ipv6 nd":
252: 		return has("interface=ether1")
253: 	}
254: 
255: 	return false
256: }

Output

<path>/source/head/docs/manual/kinds/mikrotik_chr.md</path>
<type>file</type>
<content>
1: ---
2: search:
3:   boost: 4
4: kind_code_name: mikrotik_chr
5: kind_display_name: MikroTik RouterOS CHR
6: ---
7: # -{{ kind_display_name }}-
8: 
9: [MikroTik RouterOS](https://mikrotik.com/software) Cloud Hosted Router (CHR) from the **official [`mikrotik/chr`](https://hub.docker.com/r/mikrotik/chr) image** is identified with the `-{{ kind_code_name }}-` kind in the [topology file](../topo-def-file.md). The image is published by MikroTik for `amd64` and `arm64` and is used as is: no [vrnetlab](../vrnetlab.md) build is needed.
10: 
11: The image is a QEMU VM (Alpine, qemu and the CHR disk) in a container. Its own `/start.sh` is not made for orchestrated links ([#3116](https://github.com/srl-labs/containerlab/issues/3116)), so the kind writes a launcher script into the node's lab directory, mounts it read-only and runs it as the entrypoint instead. The launcher uses only the tools that ship in the image.
12: 
13: /// note
14: The [`mikrotik_ros`](vr-ros.md) kind runs CHR from an image you build with vrnetlab. Both kinds use the same interface naming and startup config format, so a lab moves from one to the other by changing `kind` and `image`.
15: ///
16: 
17: ## Getting -{{ kind_display_name }}- image
18: 
19: ```bash
20: docker pull mikrotik/chr:7.24.5
21: ```
22: 
23: Tags follow RouterOS versions, plus `stable`, `long-term`, `testing` and `development`. This kind has been tested with 7.24.5 (stable), 7.23.8 (long-term) and 7.25rc2 (testing).
24: 
25: ## Managing -{{ kind_display_name }}- nodes
26: 
27: /// tab | CLI
28: ```bash
29: ssh admin@<node-name>
30: ```
31: ///
32: /// tab | REST API
33: ```bash
34: curl -u admin:admin http://<node-name>/rest/system/resource
35: ```
36: ///
37: /// tab | Winbox / API
38: Winbox (TCP 8291) and the RouterOS API (TCP 8728, 8729 for TLS) are reachable on the node's management address.
39: ///
40: /// tab | Console
41: The serial console is on port 5000 inside the container:
42: ```bash
43: docker exec -it <node-name> nc 127.0.0.1 5000
44: ```
45: ///
46: /// tab | bash
47: ```bash
48: docker exec -it <node-name> sh
49: ```
50: ///
51: 
52: The node's management address is RouterOS's own `ether1` address ([transparent management](#management-connectivity)), so every service RouterOS runs answers on it directly, with nothing forwarded.
53: 
54: /// note
55: Default credentials are `admin:admin`. With custom [`credentials`](../nodes.md#credentials) the kind creates that user in the `full` group and disables the factory `admin` account, which has a blank password on a fresh CHR disk.
56: ///
57: 
58: ## Interface naming
59: 
60: You can use [interface names](../topo-def-file.md#interface-naming) in the topology file like they appear in RouterOS: `etherX`, where `X` is the port number.
61: 
62: * `ether2` - first data port
63: * `ether3` - second data port, and so on
64: 
65: /// admonition
66:     type: warning
67: Data ports start at `ether2`: `ether1` is the management interface. Using `ether1` in a topology results in an error.
68: ///
69: 
70: The container interface `ethN` is RouterOS `ether(N+1)`, also when links leave gaps: a link on `eth12` alone still lands on `ether13`, with `ether2` to `ether12` present and unlinked. Interface MTU follows the link MTU, and LLDP frames are forwarded, so `/ip neighbor` shows the peers.
71: 
72: ## Node configuration
73: 
74: -{{ kind_display_name }}- nodes boot with the default CHR configuration plus the credentials, the identity (set to the node name) and the management setup described below.
75: 
76: ### Startup configuration
77: 
78: A RouterOS script (`.rsc`) set with [`startup-config`](../nodes.md#startup-config) is imported once the router is up:
79: 
80: ```yaml
81: name: chr_lab
82: topology:
83:   nodes:
84:     r1:
85:       kind: mikrotik_chr
86:       image: mikrotik/chr:7.24.5
87:       startup-config: r1.rsc
88: ```
89: 
90: The file is copied to the node's lab directory as `ftpboot/config.auto.rsc` and imported over the REST API. RouterOS imports line by line and stops at the first error: in that case the container log carries RouterOS's own reason with the line number, for example
91: 
92: ```
93: chr-launch: startup config import FAILED: Script Error: bad parameter network-type (line 3 column 59); lines before the failing one were applied, the rest were not
94: ```
95: 
96: and the node does not become ready.
97: 
98: ### Saving configuration
99: 
100: [`containerlab save`](../../cmd/save.md) writes each node's `/export` to `ftpboot/config.auto.rsc` in its lab directory. The save filter omits `add` entries tagged `clab-mgmt`, plus IPv4/IPv6 address, DHCP-client and IPv6 neighbor-discovery `add` entries for `ether1`, even without that tag. These management settings belong to the deployment and are recreated by the launcher; custom entries in those `ether1` sections are also omitted. Other interfaces and untagged configuration in other sections are preserved.
101: 
102: Discovery settings are exported explicitly, including RouterOS defaults, so an intentional `discover-interface-list=static` choice survives redeployment. The launcher-owned discovery lists are recreated before the saved configuration is imported.
103: 
104: ### Node readiness
105: 
106: The node is ready once RouterOS accepted the credentials and the startup config, reaches its gateway from the management address and, unless the configuration disables SSH, its SSH server answers. An SSH server that does not answer within `CHR_BOOT_WAIT` is logged, and the node is still declared ready, since REST and the console work. The container log then shows
107: 
108: ```
109: chr-launch: RouterOS ready
110: ```
111: 
112: and the kind's default [healthcheck](../nodes.md#healthcheck) reports the container `healthy`, so [`stages`](../nodes.md#stages) with `wait-for` can depend on it. A node whose startup config was rejected never becomes healthy. The default healthcheck's start period follows the launcher's own deadlines (`CHR_INTF_WAIT` + 3 × `CHR_BOOT_WAIT` + 90 s, 1050 s with the defaults), so a slow boot never reports `unhealthy`. A `healthcheck` set in the topology replaces the default one.
113: 
114: ### Resources
115: 
116: The kind reads the official image's own environment variables:
117: 
118: | Variable | Default | Purpose |
119: |----------|---------|---------|
120: | `MEM` | 256 MiB + 8 MiB per data interface and vCPU | VM memory (a QEMU `-m` value, e.g. `1024` or `1G`) |
121: | `CPUS` | `1` | VM vCPUs |
122: | `DISK` | `128M` | minimum disk size (the disk only grows) |
123: | `CHR_INTF_WAIT` | `60` | seconds to wait for the data interfaces containerlab attaches before the VM starts |
124: | `CHR_BOOT_WAIT` | `300` | seconds to wait for RouterOS to answer, for the startup config import, and for SSH before the node is declared ready |
125: 
126: ```yaml
127: r1:
128:   kind: mikrotik_chr
129:   image: mikrotik/chr:7.24.5
130:   env:
131:     MEM: 1024
132:     CPUS: 2
133: ```
134: 
135: KVM is used when `/dev/kvm` is available. Without it the image's QEMU runs under TCG emulation: the node works, but boots several times slower.
136: 
137: ## Management connectivity
138: 
139: Management is transparent: the IPv4 and IPv6 addresses and gateways containerlab gives the node's `eth0` are configured on RouterOS `ether1`, and the container's `eth0` is bridged to the VM's `ether1`. The router is the node's management address: SSH, the REST API, Winbox, SNMP and every other service answer on it, and the address in the topology table is the one in the RouterOS configuration.
140: 
141: The image ships busybox only (no `tc`), so where vrnetlab's [transparent management](../vrnetlab.md#management-interface) redirects with tc-mirred, this kind uses a Linux bridge. The container itself has no address on the management network; it reaches RouterOS for bootstrap over IPv6 link-local on that bridge.
142: 
143: The management return path lives in its own routing table, `mgmt`, selected by routing rules for the management addresses, and CHR's `ether1` DHCP client is disabled. A default route in the lab configuration, a PPPoE client's default route or a dual-WAN setup in `main` therefore does not cut SSH or REST access to the node. RouterOS's router advertisements on `ether1` carry a zero router lifetime, so no host on the management network takes the router as its IPv6 default gateway; the container joins the management network only once this is in place. The objects carry the comment `clab-mgmt` and are re-applied at boot when the container's addressing changed.
144: 
145: /// warning
146: Do not remove or change the `clab-mgmt` objects or the `mgmt` routing table: management of the node depends on them.
147: ///
148: 
149: ### Neighbor discovery
150: 
151: CHR routers sharing the management bridge can appear in each other's neighbor tables. LLDP, CDP and MNDP are disabled on management `ether1` by default; discovery stays enabled on the other static interfaces.
152: 
153: On first boot, before joining the management network, the launcher creates:
154: 
155: ```routeros
156: /interface list
157: add name=clab-management comment=clab-mgmt
158: add name=clab-discovery include=static exclude=clab-management comment=clab-mgmt
159: /interface list member
160: add list=clab-management interface=ether1 comment=clab-mgmt
161: /ip neighbor discovery-settings
162: set discover-interface-list=clab-discovery
163: ```
164: 
165: These reserved lists belong to the launcher. To change discovery, select another list in your startup configuration or on the running router. For example, to enable discovery on all static interfaces, including management:
166: 
167: ```routeros
168: /ip neighbor discovery-settings set discover-interface-list=static
169: ```
170: 
171: Use `discover-interface-list=none` to disable discovery everywhere, or select your own interface list. Startup configuration is imported after this default is applied. Restarts preserve the selected policy, including on nodes created before this default was introduced. SSH, REST and other management services remain available. This setting does not control IPv6 address resolution or router advertisements under `/ipv6 nd`.
172: 
173: As with RouterOS's normal discovery rules, including a bridge or bonding master enables discovery on its slave ports. If your configuration puts `ether1` into a bridge or bond, select a discovery list that excludes the master as well.
174: 
175: ## Restarts
176: 
177: [`containerlab restart`](../../cmd/restart.md) and a `/system reboot` inside RouterOS keep the interface names, MAC addresses, configuration and links: the launcher persists the NIC identity in the container on first boot and does not re-run the bootstrap. A plain `docker restart` keeps the router and its management, but Docker deletes the node's veth links, which stay down until the node is restarted with containerlab or the lab is redeployed.
178: 
179: ## File mounts
180: 
181: For each node containerlab creates in its [lab directory](../conf-artifacts.md):
182: 
183: * `ftpboot/` - mounted to `/ftpboot`, holds `config.auto.rsc`
184: * `chr-launch.sh` - the launcher, mounted read-only to `/chr-launch.sh` and rewritten on every deploy

(End of file - total 184 lines)
</content>

The launcher-owned discovery lists are recreated before the saved configuration is imported.\n\n### Node readiness\n\nThe node is ready once RouterOS accepted the credentials and the startup config, reaches its gateway from the management address and, unless the configuration disables SSH, its SSH server answers. An SSH server that does not answer within `CHR_BOOT_WAIT` is logged, and the node is still declared ready, since REST and the console work. A `healthcheck` set in the topology replaces the default one.\n\n### Resources\n\nThe kind reads the official image's own environment variables:\n\n| Variable | Default | Purpose |\n|----------|---------|---------|\n| `MEM` | 256 MiB + 8 MiB per data interface and vCPU | VM memory (a QEMU `-m` value, e.g. `1024` or `1G`) |\n| `CPUS` | `1` | VM vCPUs |\n| `DISK` | `128M` | minimum disk size (the disk only grows) |\n| `CHR_INTF_WAIT` | `60` | seconds to wait for the data interfaces containerlab attaches before the VM starts |\n| `CHR_BOOT_WAIT` | `300` | seconds to wait for RouterOS to answer, for the startup config import, and for SSH before the node is declared ready |\n\n```yaml\nr1:\n  kind: mikrotik_chr\n  image: mikrotik/chr:7.24.5\n  env:\n    MEM: 1024\n    CPUS: 2\n```\n\nKVM is used when `/dev/kvm` is available. Without it the image's QEMU runs under TCG emulation: the node works, but boots several times slower.\n\n## Management connectivity\n\nManagement is transparent: the IPv4 and IPv6 addresses and gateways containerlab gives the node's `eth0` are configured on RouterOS `ether1`, and the container's `eth0` is bridged to the VM's `ether1`. The router is the node's management address: SSH, the REST API, Winbox, SNMP and every other service answer on it, and the address in the topology table is the one in the RouterOS configuration.\n\nThe image ships busybox only (no `tc`), so where vrnetlab's [transparent management](../vrnetlab.md#management-interface) redirects with tc-mirred, this kind uses a Linux bridge. The container itself has no address on the management network; it reaches RouterOS for bootstrap over IPv6 link-local on that bridge.\n\nThe management return path lives in its own routing table, `mgmt`, selected by routing rules for the management addresses, and CHR's `ether1` DHCP client is disabled. A default route in the lab configuration, a PPPoE client's default route or a dual-WAN setup in `main` therefore does not cut SSH or REST access to the node. The objects carry the comment `clab-mgmt` and are re-applied at boot when the container's addressing changed.\n\n/// warning\nDo not remove or change the `clab-mgmt` objects or the `mgmt` routing table: management of the node depends on them.\n///\n\n### Neighbor discovery\n\nCHR routers sharing the management bridge can appear in each other's neighbor tables. LLDP, CDP and MNDP are disabled on management `ether1` by default; discovery stays enabled on the other static interfaces.\n\nOn first boot, before joining the management network, the launcher creates:\n\n```routeros\n/interface list\nadd name=clab-management comment=clab-mgmt\nadd name=clab-discovery include=static exclude=clab-management comment=clab-mgmt\n/interface list member\nadd list=clab-management interface=ether1 comment=clab-mgmt\n/ip neighbor discovery-settings\nset discover-interface-list=clab-discovery\n```\n\nThese reserved lists belong to the launcher. To change discovery, select another list in your startup configuration or on the running router. For example, to enable discovery on all static interfaces, including management:\n\n```routeros\n/ip neighbor discovery-settings set discover-interface-list=static\n```\n\nUse `discover-interface-list=none` to disable discovery everywhere, or select your own interface list. Startup configuration is imported after this default is applied. Restarts preserve the selected policy, including on nodes created before this default was introduced. SSH, REST and other management services remain available. This setting does not control IPv6 address resolution or router advertisements under `/ipv6 nd`.\n\nAs with RouterOS's normal discovery rules, including a bridge or bonding master enables discovery on its slave ports. If your configuration puts `ether1` into a bridge or bond, select a discovery list that excludes the master as well.\n\n## Restarts\n\n[`containerlab restart`](../../cmd/restart.md) and a `/system reboot` inside RouterOS keep the interface names, MAC addresses, configuration and links: the launcher persists the NIC identity in the container on first boot and does not re-run the bootstrap. A plain `docker restart` keeps the router and its management, but Docker deletes the node's veth links, which stay down until the node is restarted with containerlab or the lab is redeployed.\n\n## File mounts\n\nFor each node containerlab creates in its [lab directory](../conf-artifacts.md):\n\n* `ftpboot/` - mounted to `/ftpboot`, holds `config.auto.rsc`\n* `chr-launch.sh` - the launcher, mounted read-only to `/chr-launch.sh` and rewritten on every deploy",

1: #!/bin/sh
2: # chr-launch.sh: run the OFFICIAL mikrotik/chr image (MikroTik RouterOS Cloud
3: # Hosted Router) as a containerlab node, image untouched.
4: #
5: # The image is MikroTik's own Docker Hub distribution (Alpine + qemu +
6: # /diskimage/chr-<ver>.img + /start.sh). This script is bind-mounted into the
7: # node and set as its entrypoint by the containerlab `mikrotik_chr` kind. It replaces
8: # the image's /start.sh, which is not orchestration-ready (containerlab#3116):
9: # it scans interfaces once before containerlab attaches the links, bridges the
10: # management NIC so the container IP never reaches the router, and imports no
11: # startup config.
12: #
13: # What it does (vrnetlab's mikrotik/routeros launch.py is the model, re-done
14: # with the image's own busybox + qemu: no python, no ftp client, no tc):
15: #   1. waits for the CLAB_INTFS data interfaces containerlab attaches (eth1..N)
16: #   2. management, transparent: the container's eth0 is bridged with the VM's
17: #      ether1 tap, and eth0's addresses and default gateways move into RouterOS
18: #      ether1. The router IS the node's management address: management services
19: #      reach it directly, nothing is port-forwarded. The image ships only
20: #      busybox (no tc), so this uses a bridge where vrnetlab uses tc-mirred
21: #   3. data: one tap per container interface, bridged pairwise (ethN <-> tapN,
22: #      LLDP forwarded). NIC order in the VM follows the eth index, so container
23: #      ethN is always RouterOS ether(N+1), even with gaps
24: #   4. after boot, over IPv6 link-local on the management bridge (no address is
25: #      added to the management network): sets the credentials and the identity,
26: #      configures ether1 with the management addresses and keeps their return
27: #      path in its own routing table (`mgmt`, below), then fetches
28: #      /ftpboot/config.auto.rsc (the containerlab startup-config, when present)
29: #      from the launcher and `/import`s it
30: #   5. prints the ready marker on the container log and waits on qemu
31: #
32: # Restart (docker restart, containerlab restart --node): RouterOS keys an
33: # ethernet interface on its MAC. A NIC that comes back with a new MAC is a NEW
34: # device (ether1 becomes ether3) and the DHCP client bound to ether1 never
35: # binds. Docker re-rolls the container's eth0 MAC whenever the netns is
36: # recreated, so the node id that seeds every NIC MAC and the data NIC count are
37: # persisted in the container on the first boot and reused: the restarted qemu
38: # carries the same NICs in the same order and RouterOS keeps ether1..N and
39: # their config. The REST bootstrap is skipped on a restart; the management
40: # step re-runs and re-applies the container's current addressing.
41: #
42: # Architectures: the image's own qemu (qemu-system-x86_64 on amd64,
43: #   qemu-system-aarch64 + edk2 on arm64), KVM when /dev/kvm exists, else TCG.
44: # Args (the kind's Cmd): --hostname H [--username U --password P]
45: #   (unknown flags are ignored); credentials default to the USERNAME/PASSWORD env
46: # Env (all optional, set under the node's `env:`); MEM, CPUS and DISK are the
47: #   official image's own variables:
48: #   MEM (qemu -m value, default 256 MiB + 8 MiB per data NIC and vCPU), CPUS (default 1),
49: #   DISK (default 128M), CHR_INTF_WAIT (s, 60), CHR_BOOT_WAIT (s, 300)
50: # Serial console: port 5000 inside the container
51: #   (docker exec -it <node> nc 127.0.0.1 5000); the container itself has no
52: #   address on the management network.
53: # Ready: /.chr-launch-ready exists once RouterOS is ready (the kind's default
54: #   healthcheck); it is removed at every start.
55: # Log lines (docker logs clab-<lab>-<node>), all prefixed "chr-launch:":
56: #   "RouterOS ready"                   login + startup config done
57: #   "startup config import FAILED: …"  the router rejected the .rsc (RouterOS's
58: #                                      own reason, with the line number)
59: #   "credential bootstrap FAILED: …"   the user/password step failed
60: #   "management setup FAILED: …"       the ether1 address / mgmt table step failed

120: disk_bytes() {

123:         *M|*m) echo $(( ${1%[Mm]} * 1024 * 1024 )) ;;

161: # MACs: unique per node AND per NIC. The container's eth0 MAC is unique per
162: # node, so its LAST TWO octets identify the node; the NIC's last byte is the
163: # interface index (00 = mgmt, 01 = ether2, …). Two nodes sharing a NIC MAC on
164: # a link drop each other's frames as their own. The id is read on the FIRST
165: # boot only and persisted (see Restart in the header).
166: node_id="$(cat "$ID_FILE" 2>/dev/null)"
167: case "$node_id" in

172:             o2="${e##*:}"; rest_mac="${e%:*}"; o1="${rest_mac##*:}"
173:             node_id="$o1:$o2"
174:         else

183: # ---- management: eth0 + tap0 on one bridge, eth0's addressing to the VM ----
184: ip_global() { # ip_global <4|6> <dev>: the first global address/prefix
185:     ip -"$1" addr show dev "$2" 2>/dev/null \
186:         | sed -n '/scope global/s/^ *inet6\{0,1\} \([^ ]*\) .*/\1/p' | head -n1

188: ip_gw() { # ip_gw <4|6>: the default gateway
189:     ip -"$1" route show default 2>/dev/null | sed -n 's/^default via \([^ ]*\) .*/\1/p' | head -n1
190: }
191: M4="$(ip_global 4 eth0)"; M6="$(ip_global 6 eth0)"
192: G4="$(ip_gw 4)"; G6="$(ip_gw 6)"
193: if [ -n "$M4$M6" ]; then

213:     ip link set "$MGMT_BR" mtu "$_mtu"

216:     # DHCP discovers) reaches the management network.

218:     sysctl -qw net.ipv6.conf.eth0.disable_ipv6=1 net.ipv6.conf.tap0.disable_ipv6=1 \

220:         "net.ipv6.conf.$MGMT_BR.router_solicitations=0" 2>/dev/null
221: fi

223: ip link set "$MGMT_BR" up
224: # RouterOS's ether1 link-local address, from the MAC this script gives it
225: VM_LL="fe80::5054:ff:fe${node_id%%:*}:${node_id##*:}00"
226: VM_EP="$VM_LL%$MGMT_BR"
227: # The bridge's own link-local address (the startup-config fetch source). Read
228: # once qemu runs: until then tap0, the bridge's only port, has no carrier and
229: # the address stays tentative.
230: LL=""
231: bridge_ll() {
232:     _w=0
233:     while [ -z "$LL" ] && [ "$_w" -lt 20 ]; do
234:         LL="$(ip -6 addr show dev "$MGMT_BR" 2>/dev/null | grep -v tentative \
235:             | sed -n 's/^ *inet6 \(fe80::[^/]*\)\/64 .*/\1/p' | head -n1)"
236:         [ -n "$LL" ] || { sleep 1; _w=$((_w + 1)); }
237:     done
238:     [ -n "$LL" ] || log "no IPv6 link-local on $MGMT_BR"
239: }
240: 
241: # NIC order = PCI order = the order of the -device args below. Walk
242: # eth1..ethMAX so a gap (links on ether2 and ether5 only) keeps RouterOS
243: # numbering aligned: a missing ethN still gets a tap, just no bridge. The count
244: # never shrinks across restarts: a `docker restart` recreates the netns and
245: # every lab veth of the node is gone, and a NIC absent for one boot loses the
246: # config bound to it. The tap keeps ether(N+1) a real interface, its link comes
247: # back when containerlab restores the veth or the lab is redeployed.
248: max=0
249: for d in "$SYSNET"/eth[1-9]*; do
250:     [ -e "$d" ] || continue
251:     n="${d##*/eth}"
252:     [ "$n" -gt "$max" ] && max="$n"
253: done
254: prev="$(cat "$NICS_FILE" 2>/dev/null)"
255: case "$prev" in *[!0-9]*|"") prev=0 ;; esac
256: [ "$prev" -gt "$max" ] && max="$prev"
257: # The wait above can end short of CLAB_INTFS on a large lab (links attach after
258: # every container started): the NIC set still spans the expected count, and
259: # the watcher bridges the late veths.
260: case "$INTFS" in *[!0-9]*|"") INTFS=0 ;; esac
261: [ "$INTFS" -gt "$max" ] && max="$INTFS"
262: [ "$max" -gt "$prev" ] && { printf '%s\n' "$max" > "$NICS_FILE" 2>/dev/null || true; }
263: 
264: # RouterOS v7 memory ≈ 256 + 8·CPUS·(ifaces) MiB (MikroTik CHR system
265: # requirements). Scale by the HIGHEST generated NIC, not CLAB_INTFS: a sparse
266: # link on ether64 means CLAB_INTFS=1 yet 63 data NICs are created below.
267: if [ -z "${MEM:-}" ]; then
268:     MEM=$((256 + 8 * CPUS * max))
269: fi

271: bridge_eth() { # bridge_eth <i>: container ethI joins brI beside tapI (brI made once)
272:     _mtu="$(cat "$SYSNET/eth$1/mtu" 2>/dev/null || echo 1500)"
273:     if [ ! -e "$SYSNET/br$1" ]; then
274:         brctl addbr "br$1"
275:         ip link set "br$1" mtu "$_mtu"
276:         ip link set "tap$1" mtu "$_mtu"
277:         # forward LLDP (01:80:c2:00:00:0e, bit 14); STP/LACP cannot cross a bridge
278:         echo 16384 > "$SYSNET/br$1/bridge/group_fwd_mask" 2>/dev/null
279:         brctl addif "br$1" "tap$1"
280:     fi
281:     brctl addif "br$1" "eth$1"
282:     ip link set "eth$1" up
283:     ip link set "br$1" up
284: }
285: # A veth that lands or returns while qemu runs (containerlab restores a link
286: # right after a restart --node, a large lab attaches late, a peer node's
287: # redeploy recreates the pair) joins its bridge as it appears, so the RouterOS
288: # interface behind tapN gets its link back without a restart here.
289: watch_links() {
290:     _warned=""
291:     while kill -0 "$QEMU_PID" 2>/dev/null; do
292:         i=1
293:         while [ "$i" -le "$max" ]; do
294:             if [ -e "$SYSNET/eth$i" ] && [ ! -e "$SYSNET/br$i/brif/eth$i" ]; then
295:                 bridge_eth "$i" && log "link eth$i attached: bridged to tap$i"
296:             fi
297:             i=$((i + 1))
298:         done
299:         for d in "$SYSNET"/eth[1-9]*; do
300:             [ -e "$d" ] || continue
301:             n="${d##*/eth}"
302:             [ "$n" -gt "$max" ] || continue
303:             case " $_warned " in *" $n "*) continue ;; esac
304:             _warned="$_warned $n"
305:             log "link eth$n attached after qemu started with $max data NICs: no RouterOS interface for it until the node is restarted"
306:         done
307:         sleep 2
308:     done
309: }
310: 
311: i=1
312: while [ "$i" -le "$max" ]; do
313:     tap="tap$i"
314:     tunctl -t "$tap" >/dev/null 2>&1 || ip tuntap add dev "$tap" mode tap
315:     [ -e "$SYSNET/eth$i" ] && bridge_eth "$i"
316:     ip link set "$tap" up
317:     mac="$(printf '52:54:00:%s:%02x' "$node_id" "$((i & 255))")"
318:     NET="$NET -netdev tap,id=d$i,ifname=$tap,script=no,downscript=no -device virtio-net,netdev=d$i,mac=$mac"
319:     i=$((i + 1))
320: done
321: 
322: # shellcheck disable=SC2086
323: $QEMU $ACCEL -display none -monitor none \
324:     -m "$MEM" -smp "cpus=$CPUS" \
325:     -drive "file=$IMG,format=raw" \
326:     -serial telnet:0.0.0.0:5000,server,nowait \
327:     $NET &
328: QEMU_PID=$!
329: trap 'kill "$QEMU_PID" 2>/dev/null; wait "$QEMU_PID" 2>/dev/null; exit 0' TERM INT
330: log "qemu started (pid $QEMU_PID, mem ${MEM}M, cpus $CPUS, data nics $max)"
331: watch_links &

333: # ---- REST helpers ---------------------------------------------------------
334: json_str() { printf '%s' "$1" | sed 's/\\/\\\\/g; s/"/\\"/g'; }
335: rest() { # rest <user:pass> <path> [json]
336:     # Basic auth in a header, never in the URL: a password containing a URI
337:     # delimiter (#, ?, /) would change where the authority ends. REST_T is the
338:     # read timeout (default 20s).
339:     _auth="Authorization: Basic $(printf '%s' "$1" | base64 | tr -d '\n')"
340:     if [ $# -ge 3 ]; then
341:         wget -q -O- -T "${REST_T:-20}" --header "Content-Type: application/json" \
342:             --header "$_auth" --post-data "$3" "http://[$VM_EP]/rest/$2" 2>/dev/null
343:     else
344:         wget -q -O- -T "${REST_T:-20}" --header "$_auth" "http://[$VM_EP]/rest/$2" 2>/dev/null
345:     fi
346: }
347: # busybox wget discards the body of an HTTP error response, and RouterOS puts
348: # its diagnosis ONLY there ({"detail":"Script Error: … (line N column M)"}; its
349: # /log stays silent). So the calls whose failure must be readable go over a raw
350: # HTTP POST through busybox nc. busybox nc exits the moment stdin hits EOF,
351: # reply or not, and RouterOS keeps the connection open despite
352: # `Connection: close`, so the request side is held open on a fifo and closed
353: # once Content-Length bytes of body have arrived.
354: resp_complete() { # resp_complete <file>: headers + Content-Length bytes of body arrived
355:     _cl="$(grep -i -m1 '^Content-Length:' "$1" 2>/dev/null | tr -dc '0-9')"
356:     [ -n "$_cl" ] || return 1
357:     grep -q -m1 '^\r*$' "$1" 2>/dev/null || return 1
358:     [ "$(sed '1,/^\r*$/d' "$1" | wc -c | tr -d ' ')" -ge "$_cl" ]
359: }
360: rest_raw() { # rest_raw <user:pass> <path> <json> <timeout-s>  -> "<status> <body>"
361:     _f="/tmp/chr-rest.$$"; rm -f "$_f" "$_f.out"; mkfifo "$_f" || return 1
362:     timeout "$(( $4 + 5 ))" nc "$VM_EP" 80 <"$_f" >"$_f.out" 2>/dev/null &
363:     _pid=$!
364:     exec 7>"$_f"
365:     printf 'POST /rest/%s HTTP/1.1\r\nHost: [%s]\r\nAuthorization: Basic %s\r\nContent-Type: application/json\r\nContent-Length: %s\r\nConnection: close\r\n\r\n%s' \
366:         "$2" "$VM_LL" "$(printf '%s' "$1" | base64 | tr -d '\n')" \
367:         "$(printf '%s' "$3" | wc -c | tr -d ' ')" "$3" >&7
368:     _left=$4
369:     while kill -0 "$_pid" 2>/dev/null && ! resp_complete "$_f.out" && [ "$_left" -gt 0 ]; do
370:         sleep 1; _left=$(( _left - 1 ))
371:     done
372:     exec 7>&-
373:     wait "$_pid" 2>/dev/null
374:     _st="$(head -n1 "$_f.out" 2>/dev/null | tr -d '\r' | cut -d' ' -f2)"
375:     _body="$(sed '1,/^\r*$/d' "$_f.out" 2>/dev/null | tr -d '\r')"
376:     rm -f "$_f" "$_f.out"
377:     printf '%s %s' "${_st:-000}" "$_body"
378: }
379: # RouterOS REST returns the object/array on success and an empty body for a
380: # bare `set`: only an explicit error object is a failure here. A silently
381: # failed call is caught by the read-back checks that follow each step.
382: is_err() { case "$1" in *'"error"'*) return 0 ;; *) return 1 ;; esac; }
383: 
384: # ---- wait for RouterOS ----------------------------------------------------
385: waited=0
386: AUTH=""
387: # A fresh official CHR disk has ONLY the factory `admin` account (blank
388: # password): the first boot authenticates as that account whatever --username
389: # asks for, and the requested user is created below. On a restart the pair that
390: # answers is the requested user.
391: while [ -z "$AUTH" ]; do
392:     for _cred in "admin:" "admin:$PASSWORD" "$USERNAME:$PASSWORD"; do
393:         rest "$_cred" system/resource >/dev/null 2>&1 && { AUTH="$_cred"; break; }
394:     done
395:     [ -n "$AUTH" ] && break
396:     if ! kill -0 "$QEMU_PID" 2>/dev/null; then
397:         log "qemu exited during boot"
398:         exit 1
399:     fi
400:     sleep 2

So the calls whose failure must be readable go over a raw\n350: # HTTP POST through busybox nc. busybox nc exits the moment stdin hits EOF,\n351: # reply or not, and RouterOS keeps the connection open despite\n352: # `Connection: close`, so the request side is held open on a fifo and closed\n353: # once Content-Length bytes of body have arrived.\n354: resp_complete() { # resp_complete <file>: headers + Content-Length bytes of body arrived\n355:     _cl=\"$(grep -i -m1 '^Content-Length:' \"$1\" 2>/dev/null | tr -dc '0-9')\"\n356:     [ -n \"$_cl\" ] || return 1\n357:     grep -q -m1 '^\\r*$' \"$1\" 2>/dev/null || return 1\n358:     [ \"$(sed '1,/^\\r*$/d' \"$1\" | wc -c | tr -d ' ')\" -ge \"$_cl\" ]\n359: }\n360: rest_raw() { # rest_raw <user:pass> <path> <json> <timeout-s>  -> \"<status> <body>\"\n361:     _f=\"/tmp/chr-rest.$$\"; rm -f \"$_f\" \"$_f.out\"; mkfifo \"$_f\" || return 1\n362:     timeout \"$(( $4 + 5 ))\" nc \"$VM_EP\" 80 <\"$_f\" >\"$_f.out\" 2>/dev/null &\n363:     _pid=$!\n364:     exec 7>\"$_f\"\n365:     printf 'POST /rest/%s HTTP/1.1\\r\\nHost: [%s]\\r\\nAuthorization: Basic %s\\r\\nContent-Type: application/json\\r\\nContent-Length: %s\\r\\nConnection: close\\r\\n\\r\\n%s' \\\n366:         \"$2\" \"$VM_LL\" \"$(printf '%s' \"$1\" | base64 | tr -d '\\n')\" \\\n367:         \"$(printf '%s' \"$3\" | wc -c | tr -d ' ')\" \"$3\" >&7\n368:     _left=$4\n369:     while kill -0 \"$_pid\" 2>/dev/null && ! resp_complete \"$_f.out\" && [ \"$_left\" -gt 0 ]; do\n370:         sleep 1; _left=$(( _left - 1 ))\n371:     done\n372:     exec 7>&-\n373:     wait \"$_pid\" 2>/dev/null\n374:     _st=\"$(head -n1 \"$_f.out\" 2>/dev/null | tr -d '\\r' | cut -d' ' -f2)\"\n375:     _body=\"$(sed '1,/^\\r*$/d' \"$_f.out\" 2>/dev/null | tr -d '\\r')\"\n376:     rm -f \"$_f\" \"$_f.out\"\n377:     printf '%s %s' \"${_st:-000}\" \"$_body\"\n378: }\n379: # RouterOS REST returns the object/array on success and an empty body for a\n380: # bare `set`: only an explicit error object is a failure here. A silently\n381: # failed call is caught by the read-back checks that follow each step.\n382: is_err() { case \"$1\" in *'\"error\"'*) return 0 ;; *) return 1 ;; esac; }\n383: \n384: # ---- wait for RouterOS ----------------------------------------------------\n385: waited=0\n386: AUTH=\"\"\n387: # A fresh official CHR disk has ONLY the factory `admin` account (blank\n388: # password): the first boot authenticates as that account whatever --username\n389: # asks for, and the requested user is created below. On a restart the pair that\n390: # answers is the requested user.\n391: while [ -z \"$AUTH\" ]; do\n392:     for _cred in \"admin:\" \"admin:$PASSWORD\" \"$USERNAME:$PASSWORD\"; do\n393:         rest \"$_cred\" system/resource >/dev/null 2>&1 && { AUTH=\"$_cred\"; break; }\n394:     done\n395:     [ -n \"$AUTH\" ] && break\n396:     if ! kill -0 \"$QEMU_PID\" 2>/dev/null; then\n397:         log \"qemu exited during boot\"\n398:         exit 1\n399:     fi\n400:     sleep 2\n\n(Showing lines 1-400 of 695. Use offset=401 to continue.)\n</content>",

This script is bind-mounted into the\n# node and set as its entrypoint by the containerlab `mikrotik_chr` kind. It replaces\n# the image's /start.sh, which is not orchestration-ready (containerlab#3116):\n# it scans interfaces once before containerlab attaches the links, bridges the\n# management NIC so the container IP never reaches the router, and imports no\n# startup config.\n#\n# What it does (vrnetlab's mikrotik/routeros launch.py is the model, re-done\n# with the image's own busybox + qemu: no python, no ftp client, no tc):\n#   1. waits for the CLAB_INTFS data interfaces containerlab attaches (eth1..N)\n#   2. management, transparent: the container's eth0 is bridged with the VM's\n#      ether1 tap, and eth0's addresses and default gateways move into RouterOS\n#      ether1.         "text": "#!/bin/sh\n# chr-launch.sh: run the OFFICIAL mikrotik/chr image (MikroTik RouterOS Cloud\n# Hosted Router) as a containerlab node, image untouched.\n#\n# The image is MikroTik's own Docker Hub distribution (Alpine + qemu +\n# /diskimage/chr-<ver>.img + /start.sh). This script is bind-mounted into the\n# node and set as its entrypoint by the containerlab `mikrotik_chr` kind. It replaces\n# the image's /start.sh, which is not orchestration-ready (containerlab#3116):\n# it scans interfaces once before containerlab attaches the links, bridges the\n# management NIC so the container IP never reaches the router, and imports no\n# startup config.\n#\n# What it does (vrnetlab's mikrotik/routeros launch.py is the model, re-done\n# with the image's own busybox + qemu: no python, no ftp client, no tc):\n#   1. waits for the CLAB_INTFS data interfaces containerlab attaches (eth1..N)\n#   2. management, transparent: the container's eth0 is bridged with the VM's\n#      ether1 tap, and eth0's addresses and default gateways move into RouterOS\n#      ether1. The router IS the node's management address: management services\n#      reach it directly, nothing is port-forwarded. The image ships only\n#      busybox (no tc), so this uses a bridge where vrnetlab uses tc-mirred\n#   3. data: one tap per container interface, bridged pairwise (ethN <-> tapN,\n#      LLDP forwarded). NIC order in the VM follows the eth index, so container\n#      ethN is always RouterOS ether(N+1), even with gaps\n#   4. after boot, over IPv6 link-local on the management bridge (no address is\n#      added to the management network): sets the credentials and the identity,\n#      configures ether1 with the management addresses and keeps their return\n#      path in its own routing table (`mgmt`, below), then fetches\n#      /ftpboot/config.auto.rsc (the containerlab startup-config, when present)\n#      from the launcher and `/import`s it\n#   5. prints the ready marker on the container log and waits on qemu\n#\n# Restart (docker restart, containerlab restart --node): RouterOS keys an\n# ethernet interface on its MAC. A NIC that comes back with a new MAC is a NEW\n# device (ether1 becomes ether3) and the DHCP client bound to ether1 never\n# binds. Docker re-rolls the container's eth0 MAC whenever the netns is\n# recreated, so the node id that seeds every NIC MAC and the data NIC count are\n# persisted in the container on the first boot and reused: the restarted qemu\n# carries the same NICs in the same order and RouterOS keeps ether1..N and\n# their config. The REST bootstrap is skipped on a restart; the management\n# step re-runs and re-applies the container's current addressing.\n#\n# Architectures: the image's own qemu (qemu-system-x86_64 on amd64,\n#   qemu-system-aarch64 + edk2 on arm64), KVM when /dev/kvm exists, else TCG.\n# Args (the kind's Cmd): --hostname H [--username U --password P]\n#   (unknown flags are ignored); credentials default to the USERNAME/PASSWORD env\n# Env (all optional, set under the node's `env:`); MEM, CPUS and DISK are the\n#   official image's own variables:\n#   MEM (qemu -m value, default 256 MiB + 8 MiB per data NIC and vCPU), CPUS (default 1),\n#   DISK (default 128M), CHR_INTF_WAIT (s, 60), CHR_BOOT_WAIT (s, 300)\n# Serial console: port 5000 inside the container\n#   (docker exec -it <node> nc 127.0.0.1 5000); the container itself has no\n#   address on the management network.\n# Ready: /.chr-launch-ready exists once RouterOS is ready (the kind's default\n#   healthcheck); it is removed at every start.\n# Log lines (docker logs clab-<lab>-<node>), all prefixed \"chr-launch:\":\n#   \"RouterOS ready\"                   login + startup config done\n#   \"startup config import FAILED: …\"  the router rejected the .rsc (RouterOS's\n#                                      own reason, with the line number)\n#   \"credential bootstrap FAILED: …\"   the user/password step failed\n#   \"management setup FAILED: …\"       the ether1 address / mgmt table step failed\n#\n# Copyright 2026 NetPilot Labs\n# Licensed under the BSD 3-Clause License.\n# SPDX-License-Identifier: BSD-3-Clause\n\nset -u\n\n# Credentials come from the USERNAME/PASSWORD env the kind sets (no quoting\n# limits on the password); --username/--password still override them.\nUSERNAME=\"${USERNAME:-admin}\"\nPASSWORD=\"${PASSWORD-admin}\"\nHOSTNAME_ARG=\"\"\nwhile [ $# -gt 0 ]; do\n    case \"$1\" in\n        --username) USERNAME=\"$2\"; shift 2 ;;\n        --password) PASSWORD=\"$2\"; shift 2 ;;\n        --hostname) HOSTNAME_ARG=\"$2\"; shift 2 ;;\n        --connection-mode) shift 2 ;;\n        --trace) shift ;;\n        *) shift ;;\n    esac\ndone\n[ -n \"$HOSTNAME_ARG\" ] || HOSTNAME_ARG=\"$(hostname)\"\n\nCPUS=\"${CPUS:-1}\"\nDISK=\"${DISK:-128M}\"\nINTF_WAIT=\"${CHR_INTF_WAIT:-60}\"\nBOOT_WAIT=\"${CHR_BOOT_WAIT:-300}\"\nINTFS=\"${CLAB_INTFS:-0}\"\nSTARTUP_CFG=/ftpboot/config.auto.rsc\n# Per-container state in the rw layer: survives every restart, gone with the\n# container (destroy, redeploy --cleanup).\nDONE_MARKER=/.chr-launch-bootstrapped\nREADY_FILE=/.chr-launch-ready\nID_FILE=/.chr-launch-node-id\nNICS_FILE=/.chr-launch-nics\nMGMT_TABLE=mgmt\n# every management object the launcher creates carries this comment, so each\n# boot can find and re-apply them, and SaveConfig can leave them out\nMGMT_COMMENT=clab-mgmt\nMGMT_BR=br0\n# eth0's addressing as last seen: a launcher re-run in the same network\n# namespace finds eth0 already bridged and bare\nMGMT_FILE=/.chr-launch-mgmt\nSYSNET=/sys/class/net\n\nlog() { echo \"chr-launch: $*\"; }\nrm -f \"$READY_FILE\"\n\nFETCH_PORT=8080\n\n# ---- disk -----------------------------------------------------------------\nIMG=\"\"\nfor f in /diskimage/*.img; do [ -f \"$f\" ] && IMG=\"$f\" && break; done\nif [ -z \"$IMG\" ]; then\n    log \"no /diskimage/*.img in this image: not a RouterOS CHR image\"\n    exit 1\nfi\n# Only GROW the disk to the floor: an image already larger keeps its tail.\ndisk_bytes() {\n    case \"$1\" in\n        *G|*g) echo $(( ${1%[Gg]} * 1024 * 1024 * 1024 )) ;;\n        *M|*m) echo $(( ${1%[Mm]} * 1024 * 1024 )) ;;\n        *K|*k) echo $(( ${1%[Kk]} * 1024 )) ;;\n        *) echo \"$1\" ;;\n    esac\n}\nwant_bytes=\"$(disk_bytes \"$DISK\")\"\ncur_bytes=\"$(stat -c %s \"$IMG\" 2>/dev/null || wc -c < \"$IMG\")\"\nif [ \"$cur_bytes\" -lt \"$want_bytes\" ] 2>/dev/null; then\n    truncate -s \"$DISK\" \"$IMG\"\nfi\n\n# ---- wait for containerlab's data interfaces ------------------------------\n# shellcheck disable=SC2012  # interface names are ethN, ls is safe here\ncount_intfs() { ls -d \"$SYSNET\"/eth[1-9]* 2>/dev/null | wc -l; }\nwaited=0\nwhile [ \"$(count_intfs)\" -lt \"$INTFS\" ] && [ \"$waited\" -lt \"$INTF_WAIT\" ]; do\n    sleep 1\n    waited=$((waited + 1))\ndone\nlog \"interfaces: $(count_intfs) of $INTFS expected after ${waited}s\"\n\n# ---- qemu command line ----------------------------------------------------\n# The image's own qemu and firmware, as its /start.sh selects them.\ncase \"$(uname -m)\" in\n    aarch64|arm64)\n        QEMU=\"qemu-system-aarch64 -M virt -pflash /usr/share/qemu/edk2-aarch64-code.fd\"\n        TCG_CPU=\"-cpu cortex-a57\" ;;\n    *)\n        QEMU=\"qemu-system-x86_64\"\n        TCG_CPU=\"\" ;;\nesac\nif [ -e /dev/kvm ]; then\n    ACCEL=\"-cpu host -accel kvm\"\nelse\n    ACCEL=\"$TCG_CPU -accel tcg,split-wx=on\"\n    log \"no /dev/kvm: running under TCG emulation (slow boot)\"\nfi\n\n# MACs: unique per node AND per NIC. The container's eth0 MAC is unique per\n# node, so its LAST TWO octets identify the node; the NIC's last byte is the\n# interface index (00 = mgmt, 01 = ether2, …). Two nodes sharing a NIC MAC on\n# a link drop each other's frames as their own. The id is read on the FIRST\n# boot only and persisted (see Restart in the header).\nnode_id=\"$(cat \"$ID_FILE\" 2>/dev/null)\"\ncase \"$node_id\" in\n    [0-9a-f][0-9a-f]:[0-9a-f][0-9a-f]) ;;\n    *)\n        e=\"$(cat \"$SYSNET/eth0/address\" 2>/dev/null)\"\n        if [ -n \"$e\" ]; then\n            o2=\"${e##*:}\"; rest_mac=\"${e%:*}\"; o1=\"${rest_mac##*:}\"\n            node_id=\"$o1:$o2\"\n        else\n            node_id=\"$(hexdump -n2 -e '2/1 \"%02x:\"' /dev/urandom | sed 's/:$//')\"\n        fi\n        printf '%s\\n' \"$node_id\" > \"$ID_FILE\" 2>/dev/null \\\n            || log \"node id $node_id not persisted: a restart will renumber the NICs\"\n        ;;\nesac\nNET=\"-netdev tap,id=mgmt,ifname=tap0,script=no,downscript=no -device virtio-net,netdev=mgmt,mac=52:54:00:$node_id:00\"\n\n# ---- management: eth0 + tap0 on one bridge, eth0's addressing to the VM ----\nip_global() { # ip_global <4|6> <dev>: the first global address/prefix\n    ip -\"$1\" addr show dev \"$2\" 2>/dev/null \\\n        | sed -n '/scope global/s/^ *inet6\\{0,1\\} \\([^ ]*\\) .*/\\1/p' | head -n1\n}\nip_gw() { # ip_gw <4|6>: the default gateway\n    ip -\"$1\" route show default 2>/dev/null | sed -n 's/^default via \\([^ ]*\\) .*/\\1/p' | head -n1\n}\nM4=\"$(ip_global 4 eth0)\"; M6=\"$(ip_global 6 eth0)\"\nG4=\"$(ip_gw 4)\"; G6=\"$(ip_gw 6)\"\nif [ -n \"$M4$M6\" ]; then\n    printf 'M4=%s\\nM6=%s\\nG4=%s\\nG6=%s\\n' \"$M4\" \"$M6\" \"$G4\" \"$G6\" > \"$MGMT_FILE\" 2>/dev/null || true\nelif [ -f \"$MGMT_FILE\" ]; then\n    # shellcheck disable=SC1090  # written by this script, four plain assignments\n    . \"$MGMT_FILE\"\nfi\nlog \"management: ${M4:-no IPv4}${M6:+, $M6} via ${G4:-no gateway}${G6:+, $G6}\"\n\ntunctl -t tap0 >/dev/null 2>&1 || ip tuntap add dev tap0 mode tap\nif [ ! -e \"$SYSNET/$MGMT_BR\" ]; then\n    _mtu=\"$(cat \"$SYSNET/eth0/mtu\" 2>/dev/null || echo 1500)\"\n    ip addr flush dev eth0\n    # The launcher's side of the bootstrap channel: IPv6 link-local on the\n    # bridge only. eth0 and tap0 are plain bridge ports.\n    sysctl -qw net.ipv6.conf.default.disable_ipv6=0 net.ipv6.conf.default.accept_ra=0 2>/dev/null\n    brctl addbr \"$MGMT_BR\"\n    brctl setfd \"$MGMT_BR\" 0 2>/dev/null\n    echo 16384 > \"$SYSNET/$MGMT_BR/bridge/group_fwd_mask\" 2>/dev/null\n    ip link set \"$MGMT_BR\" address \"52:54:01:$node_id:00\"\n    ip link set tap0 mtu \"$_mtu\"\n    ip link set \"$MGMT_BR\" mtu \"$_mtu\"\n    # eth0 joins the bridge only once ether1 is configured (mgmt_attach): until\n    # then nothing the router sends (its boot-time router advertisements, CHR's\n    # DHCP discovers) reaches the management network.\n    brctl addif \"$MGMT_BR\" tap0\n    sysctl -qw net.ipv6.conf.eth0.disable_ipv6=1 net.ipv6.conf.tap0.disable_ipv6=1 \\\n        \"net.ipv6.conf.$MGMT_BR.disable_ipv6=0\" \"net.ipv6.conf.$MGMT_BR.accept_ra=0\" \\\n        \"net.ipv6.conf.$MGMT_BR.router_solicitations=0\" 2>/dev/null\nfi\nip link set tap0 up\nip link set \"$MGMT_BR\" up\n# RouterOS's ether1 link-local address, from the MAC this script gives it\nVM_LL=\"fe80::5054:ff:fe${node_id%%:*}:${node_id##*:}00\"\nVM_EP=\"$VM_LL%$MGMT_BR\"\n# The bridge's own link-local address (the startup-config fetch source). Read\n# once qemu runs: until then tap0, the bridge's only port, has no carrier and\n# the address stays tentative.\nLL=\"\"\nbridge_ll() {\n    _w=0\n    while [ -z \"$LL\" ] && [ \"$_w\" -lt 20 ]; do\n        LL=\"$(ip -6 addr show dev \"$MGMT_BR\" 2>/dev/null | grep -v tentative \\\n            | sed -n 's/^ *inet6 \\(fe80::[^/]*\\)\\/64 .*/\\1/p' | head -n1)\"\n        [ -n \"$LL\" ] || { sleep 1; _w=$((_w + 1)); }\n    done\n    [ -n \"$LL\" ] || log \"no IPv6 link-local on $MGMT_BR\"\n}\n\n# NIC order = PCI order = the order of the -device args below. Walk\n# eth1..ethMAX so a gap (links on ether2 and ether5 only) keeps RouterOS\n# numbering aligned: a missing ethN still gets a tap, just no bridge. The count\n# never shrinks across restarts: a `docker restart` recreates the netns and\n# every lab veth of the node is gone, and a NIC absent for one boot loses the\n# config bound to it. The tap keeps ether(N+1) a real interface, its link comes\n# back when containerlab restores the veth or the lab is redeployed.\nmax=0\nfor d in \"$SYSNET\"/eth[1-9]*; do\n    [ -e \"$d\" ] || continue\n    n=\"${d##*/eth}\"\n    [ \"$n\" -gt \"$max\" ] && max=\"$n\"\ndone\nprev=\"$(cat \"$NICS_FILE\" 2>/dev/null)\"\ncase \"$prev\" in *[!0-9]*|\"\") prev=0 ;; esac\n[ \"$prev\" -gt \"$max\" ] && max=\"$prev\"\n# The wait above can end short of CLAB_INTFS on a large lab (links attach after\n# every container started): the NIC set still spans the expected count, and\n# the watcher bridges the late veths.\ncase \"$INTFS\" in *[!0-9]*|\"\") INTFS=0 ;; esac\n[ \"$INTFS\" -gt \"$max\" ] && max=\"$INTFS\"\n[ \"$max\" -gt \"$prev\" ] && { printf '%s\\n' \"$max\" > \"$NICS_FILE\" 2>/dev/null || true; }\n\n# RouterOS v7 memory ≈ 256 + 8·CPUS·(ifaces) MiB (MikroTik CHR system\n# requirements). Scale by the HIGHEST generated NIC, not CLAB_INTFS: a sparse\n# link on ether64 means CLAB_INTFS=1 yet 63 data NICs are created below.\nif [ -z \"${MEM:-}\" ]; then\n    MEM=$((256 + 8 * CPUS * max))\nfi\n\nbridge_eth() { # bridge_eth <i>: container ethI joins brI beside tapI (brI made once)\n    _mtu=\"$(cat \"$SYSNET/eth$1/mtu\" 2>/dev/null || echo 1500)\"\n    if [ ! -e \"$SYSNET/br$1\" ]; then\n        brctl addbr \"br$1\"\n        ip link set \"br$1\" mtu \"$_mtu\"\n        ip link set \"tap$1\" mtu \"$_mtu\"\n        # forward LLDP (01:80:c2:00:00:0e, bit 14); STP/LACP cannot cross a bridge\n        echo 16384 > \"$SYSNET/br$1/bridge/group_fwd_mask\" 2>/dev/null\n        brctl addif \"br$1\" \"tap$1\"\n    fi\n    brctl addif \"br$1\" \"eth$1\"\n    ip link set \"eth$1\" up\n    ip link set \"br$1\" up\n}\n# A veth that lands or returns while qemu runs (containerlab restores a link\n# right after a restart --node, a large lab attaches late, a peer node's\n# redeploy recreates the pair) joins its bridge as it appears, so the RouterOS\n# interface behind tapN gets its link back without a restart here.\nwatch_links() {\n    _warned=\"\"\n    while kill -0 \"$QEMU_PID\" 2>/dev/null; do\n        i=1\n        while [ \"$i\" -le \"$max\" ]; do\n            if [ -e \"$SYSNET/eth$i\" ] && [ ! -e \"$SYSNET/br$i/brif/eth$i\" ]; then\n                bridge_eth \"$i\" && log \"link eth$i attached: bridged to tap$i\"\n            fi\n            i=$((i + 1))\n        done\n        for d in \"$SYSNET\"/eth[1-9]*; do\n            [ -e \"$d\" ] || continue\n            n=\"${d##*/eth}\"\n            [ \"$n\" -gt \"$max\" ] || continue\n            case \" $_warned \" in *\" $n \"*) continue ;; esac\n            _warned=\"$_warned $n\"\n            log \"link eth$n attached after qemu started with $max data NICs: no RouterOS interface for it until the node is restarted\"\n        done\n        sleep 2\n    done\n}\n\ni=1\nwhile [ \"$i\" -le \"$max\" ]; do\n    tap=\"tap$i\"\n    tunctl -t \"$tap\" >/dev/null 2>&1 || ip tuntap add dev \"$tap\" mode tap\n    [ -e \"$SYSNET/eth$i\" ] && bridge_eth \"$i\"\n    ip link set \"$tap\" up\n    mac=\"$(printf '52:54:00:%s:%02x' \"$node_id\" \"$((i & 255))\")\"\n    NET=\"$NET -netdev tap,id=d$i,ifname=$tap,script=no,downscript=no -device virtio-net,netdev=d$i,mac=$mac\"\n    i=$((i + 1))\ndone\n\n# shellcheck disable=SC2086\n$QEMU $ACCEL -display none -monitor none \\\n    -m \"$MEM\" -smp \"cpus=$CPUS\" \\\n    -drive \"file=$IMG,format=raw\" \\\n    -serial telnet:0.0.0.0:5000,server,nowait \\\n    $NET &\nQEMU_PID=$!\ntrap 'kill \"$QEMU_PID\" 2>/dev/null; wait \"$QEMU_PID\" 2>/dev/null; exit 0' TERM INT\nlog \"qemu started (pid $QEMU_PID, mem ${MEM}M, cpus $CPUS, data nics $max)\"\nwatch_links &\n\n# ---- REST helpers ---------------------------------------------------------\njson_str() { printf '%s' \"$1\" | sed 's/\\\\/\\\\\\\\/g; s/\"/\\\\\"/g'; }\nrest() { # rest <user:pass> <path> [json]\n    # Basic auth in a header, never in the URL: a password containing a URI\n    # delimiter (#, ?, /) would change where the authority ends. REST_T is the\n    # read timeout (default 20s).\n    _auth=\"Authorization: Basic $(printf '%s' \"$1\" | base64 | tr -d '\\n')\"\n    if [ $# -ge 3 ]; then\n        wget -q -O- -T \"${REST_T:-20}\" --header \"Content-Type: application/json\" \\\n            --header \"$_auth\" --post-data \"$3\" \"http://[$VM_EP]/rest/$2\" 2>/dev/null\n    else\n        wget -q -O- -T \"${REST_T:-20}\" --header \"$_auth\" \"http://[$VM_EP]/rest/$2\" 2>/dev/null\n    fi\n}\n# busybox wget discards the body of an HTTP error response, and RouterOS puts\n# its diagnosis ONLY there ({\"detail\":\"Script Error: … (line N column M)\"}; its\n# /log stays silent). So the calls whose failure must be readable go over a raw\n# HTTP POST through busybox nc. busybox nc exits the moment stdin hits EOF,\n# reply or not, and RouterOS keeps the connection open despite\n# `Connection: close`, so the request side is held open on a fifo and closed\n# once Content-Length bytes of body have arrived.\nresp_complete() { # resp_complete <file>: headers + Content-Length bytes of body arrived\n    _cl=\"$(grep -i -m1 '^Content-Length:' \"$1\" 2>/dev/null | tr -dc '0-9')\"\n    [ -n \"$_cl\" ] || return 1\n    grep -q -m1 '^\\r*$' \"$1\" 2>/dev/null || return 1\n    [ \"$(sed '1,/^\\r*$/d' \"$1\" | wc -c | tr -d ' ')\" -ge \"$_cl\" ]\n}\nrest_raw() { # rest_raw <user:pass> <path> <json> <timeout-s>  -> \"<status> <body>\"\n    _f=\"/tmp/chr-rest.$$\"; rm -f \"$_f\" \"$_f.out\"; mkfifo \"$_f\" || return 1\n    timeout \"$(( $4 + 5 ))\" nc \"$VM_EP\" 80 <\"$_f\" >\"$_f.out\" 2>/dev/null &\n    _pid=$!\n    exec 7>\"$_f\"\n    printf 'POST /rest/%s HTTP/1.1\\r\\nHost: [%s]\\r\\nAuthorization: Basic %s\\r\\nContent-Type: application/json\\r\\nContent-Length: %s\\r\\nConnection: close\\r\\n\\r\\n%s' \\\n        \"$2\" \"$VM_LL\" \"$(printf '%s' \"$1\" | base64 | tr -d '\\n')\" \\\n        \"$(printf '%s' \"$3\" | wc -c | tr -d ' ')\" \"$3\" >&7\n    _left=$4\n    while kill -0 \"$_pid\" 2>/dev/null && ! resp_complete \"$_f.out\" && [ \"$_left\" -gt 0 ]; do\n        sleep 1; _left=$(( _left - 1 ))\n    done\n    exec 7>&-\n    wait \"$_pid\" 2>/dev/null\n    _st=\"$(head -n1 \"$_f.out\" 2>/dev/null | tr -d '\\r' | cut -d' ' -f2)\"\n    _body=\"$(sed '1,/^\\r*$/d' \"$_f.out\" 2>/dev/null | tr -d '\\r')\"\n    rm -f \"$_f\" \"$_f.out\"\n    printf '%s %s' \"${_st:-000}\" \"$_body\"\n}\n# RouterOS REST returns the object/array on success and an empty body for a\n# bare `set`: only an explicit error object is a failure here. A silently\n# failed call is caught by the read-back checks that follow each step.\nis_err() { case \"$1\" in *'\"error\"'*) return 0 ;; *) return 1 ;; esac; }\n\n# ---- wait for RouterOS ----------------------------------------------------\nwaited=0\nAUTH=\"\"\n# A fresh official CHR disk has ONLY the factory `admin` account (blank\n# password): the first boot authenticates as that account whatever --username\n# asks for, and the requested user is created below. 410: # Ready means a user can log in: RouterOS starts its SSH server well after REST
411: # answers on a slow boot (about 40 s later under TCG on arm64), so when the
412: # configuration keeps SSH enabled, its port must send the banner first.
413: # A config that disables SSH is not waited for; a banner that never comes is
414: # logged and the node is still declared ready (REST works).
415: ssh_banner() { (sleep 3) | nc "$VM_EP" 22 2>/dev/null | head -c 7; }
416: ready() {
417:     case "$(rest "$AUTH" "ip/service?name=ssh")" in
418:         *'"disabled":"false"'*)
419:             _t0=$(date +%s)
420:             until [ "$(ssh_banner)" = "SSH-2.0" ]; do
421:                 if [ $(( $(date +%s) - _t0 )) -ge "$BOOT_WAIT" ]; then
422:                     log "SSH did not answer within $(( $(date +%s) - _t0 ))s (REST works)"
423:                     break
424:                 fi
425:                 sleep 2
426:             done
427:             ;;
428:     esac
429:     touch "$READY_FILE" 2>/dev/null || true
430:     log "RouterOS ready"
431: }

433: # A failed step WITHHOLDS the ready marker (qemu keeps running, so the node can
434: # still be inspected over the serial console, port 5000 in the container).
435: bootstrap_fail() {
436:     log "credential bootstrap FAILED: $1"
437:     wait "$QEMU_PID"
438:     exit 0
439: }
